Document 002: 10年期国债收益率创2002年最高，法国会是欧债危机2.0的引爆点吗？

Source PDF: all report pdf/10年期国债收益率创2002年最高，法国会是欧债危机2.0的引爆点吗？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

法国财政恶化与社会动荡交织，债券市场⻛险警报持续升级： 10 年期国债收益率⼀度触及
5% ，创 2002 年以来新⾼，法德利差逼近 160 个基点。穆迪评级审查临近， 2027 年预算案
推进受阻，外国投资者加速撤离。分析⼈⼠警告，法国或成为欧元区新⼀轮主权债务危机
的⻛险源，财政紧缩与政治博弈也将考验欧洲整体稳定。
摘要：
法国正同时承受社会动荡与财政恶化的双重压⼒，债券市场已率先作出反应。
法国 10 年期国债收益率周三早间⼀度升⾄ 5% ，创 2002 年以来最⾼⽔平。
与此同时，法德 10 年期国债利差逼近 160 个基点，触及欧债危机以来最⾼点，外国投资者正加速
撤离法国国债市场。
市场的紧张情绪叠加多重催化剂：上周末爆发的极左翼团体骚乱、本周晚些时候即将到来的穆
迪主权评级审查，以及政府在推进 2027 年预算案上的持续受阻。德意志银⾏⾼级经济学家
Yacine Rouimi 在研究报告中指出，在 18 个欧元区成员国中，法国是⾯临主权债务危机⻛险最⾼
的经济体。
骚乱失控，内政部⻓请辞遭拒

上周末，法国多个⼤城市爆发由极左翼团体主导的街头骚乱，暴徒冲击并洗劫零售商店及奢侈
品⻔店，纵⽕焚烧建筑，并与警⽅发⽣激烈冲突。
内政部⻓ Laurent Nuñez 周⼀公开承认，警⽅在部分⼤都市区⼀度失去对局势的控制。 Nuñez 随
后向总统⻢克⻰提出辞职，但遭到后者拒绝。
花旗集团策略师 Jamie Searle 警告称， "OAT 与德债利差昨⽇已告破位 " ，法国的国别⻛险溢价正
进⼊ " 未知领域 " 。Searle 同时指出，上周末的骚乱进⼀步加剧了法国预算谈判的复杂程度。
财政积弊深重，评级压⼒迫近
法国的财政困境并⾮⼀朝⼀⼣。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
《世界报》编辑总监、英国《⾦融时报》专栏作家 Sylvie Kauffmann 撰⽂指出，法国⾃ 1974 年
以来从未实现财政平衡，新冠疫情后 " 不惜⼀切代价 " 的财政扩张政策更将公共⾚字推升⾄今年
GDP 的 5.4% 。⽬前，法国国家债务已达 GDP 的 119% ，为 1946 年以来最⾼⽔平，且仍在持续攀
升。
穆迪定于本周晚些时候对法国 Aa3 主权评级展开审查，该评级此前已被列⼊负⾯展望。在政治僵
局之下，⻢克⻰政府能否顺利通过 2027 年财年预算案，仍存在较⼤不确定性。债券市场的定价
已在相当程度上反映出对评级下调的担忧。
欧债危机 2.0 ⻛险浮现
德意志银⾏经济学家 Rouimi 构建的欧盟经济动荡⻛险评估框架涵盖公共财政、对外收⽀、私⼈
债务、银⾏业韧性及劳动⼒市场等多个维度。

评估结果显⽰，爱尔兰、希腊和葡萄⽛⾃ 2010 年以来⻛险状况显著改善，⽽法国、⽐利时和芬
兰则持续朝错误⽅向演变。 Rouimi 的结论是，⼀旦欧元区再度爆发系统性危机，法国可能成为
新的震源。
花旗策略师 Searle 亦对此表⽰担忧。外国投资者持续减持法国国债的趋势，正在加剧市场对流动
性和融资成本的顾虑。
福利削减难题，⼤选前景⽣变
分析⼈⼠指出，此轮骚乱的深层逻辑与法国福利体系的可持续性危机密切相关。在⾼利率环境
下，如何为庞⼤的福利国家持续融资，已成为法国财政政策的核⼼⽭盾。若不推⾏实质性财政
整顿，投资者信⼼的进⼀步流失将难以避免。
然⽽，任何紧缩措施都将⾯临来⾃左翼的强烈抵制，⽽这恰恰为极右翼候选⼈ Marine Le Pen 在
明年总统⼤选中提供了有利的⺠调空间。当前的多重危机，正在重塑法国政治格局，并对欧元
区整体稳定构成潜在威胁。

### Page 3

Document 030: 2026Q4 PCB產業-需求推動漲價，傳輸靠PTFE解方__福邦投顧_202610

### Page 2

2【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
26Q4結論-上游材料加速更新，漲價仍為主旋律
1.PCB上游:供不應求推動漲價動力，新材料應用下帶來新機會
• 玻纖布:E-Glass(高階)/LowDK-2/T-Glass 在2026Q4~2027年呈現全面緊缺，成為推動當前上游材料漲價的主要
因素，也是決定結構性缺貨的關鍵原料，玻布廠迎來產品組合改善+報價上揚雙重催化。
• 銅箔:HVLP4加速驗證日台以外的供應商，中系擴產積極+中系設備加速採用下，維持2027年供需缺口放緩預期。
• 銅箔基板:PTFE樹脂為美系客戶解決互聯的確定性解方，成為銅箔基板材料中新晉路線；傳統的PPO/CH樹脂仍
有一片天，26Q4 VR300 設計將定案，PTFE+M8/M9設計呼聲較高。
• 鑽針:高階鍍膜針需求攀升，供應商在2026~2028年加快投入建置產能，新材料(PTFE)仍有一定的鑽針消耗，產
品組合的改變遠比漲價來的重要。
2. IC載板: 產能全數售罄，缺料與缺產能並行，關注供應商漲價態度。
• 供需缺口:維持2026/2027年 20%+/40%+ 缺口預期。

• 主邏輯為面積放大+層數增加，大廠產能售罄，客戶需要付出更高的價格預定產能+料源。
3. PCB:關注AWS、Google新品放量，以及2027~2028年HDI設計的變化，聚焦Tier1供應體系和伺服器
級HDI稀缺產能。
4. 建議首選關注個股:
• 高階稀缺HDI產能:2368金像電、3037欣興、4958臻鼎
• 產品組合優化顯著:1303南亞、1815富喬、8021尖點、8039台虹
• 缺貨27~28年不緩解+AI Agent受惠:8046南電、3189景碩、3037欣興、4958 臻鼎

表1:OBBA法案和AI行動計畫
全年核心:政策主導AI為基本方針
法案 大而美法案(One Big Beautiful Bill Act) AI行動計劃(America's AI Action Plan)
內容 1. 下調聯邦所得稅:從35%下調至21%，
減輕企業稅賦負擔。
2. 恢復2017年減稅與就業法案項目中
允許企業當年資本支出可以全額抵
減稅賦(折舊扣除)，有利重資本支出
廠商加快投資回收時間，受惠廠商
如CSP大廠。
3. 國內R&D全額費用化: 為了獎勵企業
在美國進行生產研發，符合條件的
R&D費用可以於當年度全面費用化，
受惠廠商如Open AI、Anthropic等。
1. 加速AI創新:確保美國擁有全球最強
大的AI系統，放鬆相關管制與障礙，
並鼓勵開源等。
2. 增加AI基礎設施建置:提升美國AI算
力基礎設施硬體基礎，以滿足AI推
動經濟和安全的需求；加快關鍵項
目的審核，加快建設資料中心、晶
圓代工廠、電力基礎設施，掃除一

切AI基礎設施擴充的障礙。
3. 維護國際外交與AI安全:在全球AI競
爭中掌握規則制定權，輸出美國AI
技術，同時阻止對手利用美國創新
超越美國，嚴防高階算力流向競爭
國家，向盟友出口美國AI算力技術
等，有利主權AI(Sovereign AI)的發
展。。
資料來源:福邦投顧整理
• AI 成為美國政策上的基本方針，OBBBA法案刺激企業加大投資AI算力，AAAP計畫掃除建
置AI算力的瓶頸和主權AI方針的確立。

20~30層，未來30~50層越顯常見。

12【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
PCB鑽針需求呈現翻倍成長-CCL材料影響
◼ PCB用鑽針受到板材影響，在同樣板厚下，每針可鑽孔次數大幅縮水:
• M6~M7時主針鑽孔壽命約600~800次
• M8材料下可鑽400~500次
• M9材料下僅剩下100~200次
• 材料越高階，鑽針使用量會是過往的6倍以上。
過去 目前 未來

### Page 13

13【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
PCB鑽針需求呈現翻倍成長-長徑比帶動報價上揚
• 當前主流GPU用PCB約5mm厚度，隨著AI用PCB板厚的提升，每增加1mm，製造上可能會採
用增加一根預鑽針，採用預鑽針預鑽至特定深度，後續用主針完成加工。
• GPU伺服器:GB200採用「1主針配2白針」→GB300採用「1主針配3白針」→VR200採用「
1主針配4白針」，
• AI用的鑽針，長度/直徑比超過30規格的鑽針，報價上會採用一個方案一個價格，因為
加工精度要求提高，難度變大；目前AI用的鍍膜鑽針報價較傳統白針多20~50%，若是
這種報價方式，特別是板厚超過7mm，產品售價將會多40~50%；未來約在2027~2028年
時，7.5~8.5mm的鑽針將成為主流，報價將會是一般白針的6倍以上報價。
• 毛利率方向:AI用的鍍膜鑽針鑽針，根據產業訪查，產品毛利率約50%+。

### Page 20

20【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
• 下個世代的半導體封裝需要更大的面積來放置更多元件、支持2D~3D封裝(CoWoS~SOIC)
，因此材料的要求需要Low CTE、高彈性變量來達到低翹曲度（Warpage)和連接可靠度
。
• 根據Resonac白皮書，為了解決內埋元件載板對於核心層的厚度精度要求，較薄的核心
層或不均勻的厚度，會導致內埋元件的損壞，因此之後許多載板核心的設計會採用厚
Core+ low CTE 膠片（採用T/Q Glass)。
• 2026年的TPU，2027年的Maia300都有見到這類核心Core CCL+PP的設計方案
(Multilayer Core)，此也會多消耗T-Glass的供應量。
T-Glass:客戶積極導入抗翹曲設計
圖5:Resonac Type F產品

### Page 30

30【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
無布VS.有布方向選擇
⚫ PTFE 由於Dk/Df的性能優於電子布，如果PTFE搭配電子布，會導致其Dk/Df轉差。
⚫ 未來走向:
1. 有布方案(M9~M10搭配LowDK2~4電子布):若PCB上須放置CPU/GPU/ASIC等晶片，考量尺寸
安定性因素，會需要搭配具有電子布的銅箔基板，例如UBB、ComputeBoard、
SwitchBoard、MotherBoard。
2. 無布方案(搭配無布膠片):若僅考連傳輸性能(不放置晶片)，目前採取的無布方案，樹
脂填矽粉/球型矽微粉的方案為主流，以改善膨脹性。
表19:PTFE與電子布的性能比較
資料來源:產業調研、福邦投顧整理
PTFE樹脂 LowDK 電子布 LowDK 3~4(1/2代Q-glass)
Dk(10Ghz) ~2.1 4.6~4.8 3.7~3.8
Df(10Ghz) ~0.0002 0.002~0.003 0.0002~0.0003
PTFE樹脂 球型矽微粉 LowDK 3~4(1/2代Q-glass)

主要以球型粉料為主。

### Page 32

32【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
PCB製造端的製程變化-銅漿燒結
⚫ 銅漿燒結製程源於瞬態液相燒結(TLPS)技術，主要用於實現PCB盲/埋孔電路層間的Z軸
互連。
⚫ 過去傳統高多層多採用背鑽的製程，但在NV陣營，開始嘗試使用”銅漿燒結“的製程來
處理高多層版的訊號連接，以解決背鑽精度控制的問題。
⚫ 目前具有【燒結製程+大板製造能力】的主要為板廠，例如深南電路、景旺電子、勝宏
科技、滬電股份、生益電子；台系則有金像電具備此製程能力。

### Page 35

38【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
OpenClaw:加速從LLM往AI Agent 發展
◼ OpenClaw:開放式AI助理項目，可以根據使用者指令在應用程式和線上服務之間自行處
理複雜任務，並被設計為可以代替使用者執行任務的AI軟體，並非過往的聊天機器人。
◼ OpenAI推出GPT5.4，此次更新提供Thinking和Pro兩種模型； Pro主要適用於需要進行
複雜任務的使用者，Thinking模型可以在產出答案前提出思考計畫，使用者可以從中調
整任務方向→強化Agent能力，可以支持智慧裝置執行複雜的工作流程。
◼ NVIDIA將推出類似OpenClaw的開放式平台-NemoClaw。
資料來源:產業調研、福邦投顧整理
表23：LLM 與 Agent 推理比較

運作本質 單向生成 循環決策

對硬體影響 主要消耗GPU算力 CPU需要介入成為主要調度，其中涉及沙

### Page 36

39【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
Agentic AI的瓶頸主要來自CPU
◼ 根據論文《A CPU-Centric Perspective on Agentic AI》一文指出，可以看出執行時間中
CPU延遲占了大比例（紅圈），最高可達到90%以上；吞吐量瓶頸除了來自GPU，亦會
來自CPU;能耗方面，大批次處理中(Batch size提升至128)，CPU平行處理效率低於GPU
，佔到總能耗超過44%，延遲會從2.8秒提升至6秒以上，超過2倍。
資料來源: 《A CPU-Centric Perspective on Agentic AI》 、福邦投顧整理
說明:Haystack RAG（問答檢索）/Toolformer（數學運算與 API 呼叫）/ChemCrow（化學文獻檢索）
Langchain（網路搜尋與摘要）/SWE-Agent（程式碼與 Bash/Python 執行)
圖11:Agent下，CPU佔據了大部分的延遲

### Page 37

40【僅供內部教育訓練使用，嚴禁外流 】
CPU朝向多核發展→推升載板面積
◼ Agent執行:API處理、資料解析、腳本執行，90%由CPU完成，為了降低延遲，CPU往多
核心發展是趨勢。
◼ 記憶體與上下文切換:CPU需要頻繁在不同Agent狀態做切換，多核心架構能讓每個
Agent有獨立的處理單元，降低上下文切換帶來的效率損耗。
◼ 沙盒隔離/安全:Agent 需要大量獨立的一般伺服器建立Sandbox來執行，使用量與使用
者和使用量正相關，需要更多核心數、高頻寬。
資料來源:Intel、AMD、福邦投顧整理預估

傳統光通訊模組板-HDI/mSAP供應體系的新機會
◼因應高度整合和小型化需求，光模組開始大量採用MSAP來縮小產品體積和損耗；進入
1.6T，L/S 要求微縮至20µm-25µm；因此1.6T開始，強制採用MSAP。
◼材料規格上，亦往M7~M8等級以上升級，但在MSAP部分，會大量採用載體銅箔(三井金屬
90%市佔率)、日東紡的Low CTE布，目前有面臨缺料的情況。

◼雲端資料中心多採用品牌+白牌的混合方案
◼Spine和DCI 因為結構複雜，所以多採用品牌廠，但Google和Amazon 由於架構
和軟體上較強，所以選擇白牌來配合他們的結構。
◼TOR和 Leaf 結構簡單，可以看到較多白牌(裸機、白盒)的方案。
◼xAI的交換機品牌主要採用Juniper的方案，主權AI體系(ex:星際之門、中東等)

交換機板:主流AI層數較低，傳統乙太網多層為主
◼ 資料中心需要承載流量大+高速傳輸，在此需求下，高多層板成為此需求的解決方案，
其優點在於電子元件間連線縮短下，訊號傳輸速度提高，而且也方遍佈線。
◼乙太網交換機為各家CSP+主權主要Scale out的主流，800G報價是400G的2倍以上，1.6T
是800G的4倍以上。

1

國際經濟金融週報內容摘要 
美 
國 
美國就業回穩支撐景氣韌性，惟通膨壓力與貿易逆差擴大增添政策不確定性 
經濟 
⚫ 8 月美國非農就業增加 16.2 萬人，優於前月及近一年平均，失業率維持
4.1%，就業市場呈現溫和增長，整體表現仍具韌性。新增就業以餐飲服
務、地方政府教育及製造業為主，惟資訊業仍減少人力。 
⚫ 7 月美國職位空缺數增至 727.1 萬個 ， 職缺率由4.3%升至 4.4%； 惟聘僱
及總離職人數分別減少 27.8 萬人及 26.5 萬人，其中主動離職人數減少
15.7 萬人，裁員與解僱人數減少11.9 萬人。整體來看，企業用人需求尚
屬穩健，但招募及勞工流動動能略有降溫，勞動市場供需仍維持平衡。 
⚫ 8 月美國 ISM 製造業採購經理人指數為 54.6，雖較上月下降 1.0 點，但
已連續八個月維持擴張。新訂單、就業、未完成訂單及進口指數回落，
顯示需求動能有所放緩 ； 惟生產與出口持續擴張 ， 供應商交貨時間延長 ，
原物料價格仍處高檔，整體製造業活動仍維持擴張態勢。 
⚫ 8 月美國 ISM 服務業採購經理人指數由 54.1 升至 55.4，連續第 26 個月
維持擴張。商業活動與新訂單指數分別升至 61.7 及 60.9，反映服務業
需求仍具支撐；惟雇傭指數連續兩個月低於 50，企業聘僱態度仍較審
慎。此外，價格指數升至 72.6，創 2022 年 8 月以來新高，通膨與成本
壓力依然偏高。 
⚫ 7 月美國商品及服務貿易逆差擴大至 886 億美元，較上月增加 24.4%，
主因出口減少 2.1%、進口增加 2.8%。出口下滑主要受原油及非貨幣黃
金等工業原料出口減少影響；進口則受電腦、半導體等資本財需求增加
帶動。累計前 7 月貿易逆差較去年同期縮減 29.6%，顯示美國對外貿易
失衡情況明顯改善。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 美國於 8 月 30 日以伊朗革命衛隊疑似準備在荷姆茲海峽部署水雷、威
脅商業航運安全為由，對伊朗拉拉克島軍事設施發動精準打擊。伊朗隨
後向約旦境內美軍基地發射飛彈及無人機報復，區域緊張情勢明顯升
溫。由於荷姆茲海峽為全球重要原油運輸樞紐，市場對能源供應與航運
安全的疑慮加深，帶動國際油價及避險需求走高，並增添全球通膨及金
融市場不確定性。 
短評 ⚫ 8 月非農就業優於預期 ， 失業率維持低檔 ， 勞動市場尚未明顯轉弱 ；7 月

職缺增加且裁員率下降，反映企業用人需求仍具支撐。另一方面，製造
業與服務業 PMI 持續位於擴張區間，其中服務業需求動能尤為強勁。
然而，製造業及服務業價格指數維持高檔、貿易逆差擴大，加上關稅政
策與中東地緣政治風險升溫，可能推升通膨壓力，市場對聯準會維持偏
緊貨幣政策的預期有所升高。 
歐 
元 
區 
通膨壓力回升，核心通膨走向值得關注 
經濟 ⚫ 歐元區 7 月失業率持平 6.4%。 ⚫ 歐元區 8 月 HICP 通膨上升 0.4 個百分點至 3.3%。

事件 
⚫ 歐盟執委會於 8 月 31 日公布鋼鐵產品原產地認定新規定，要求進口商
提供「熔煉及澆鑄地」證明文件，以確認鋼鐵真正來源國，防堵洗產地
情形。 
短評 
⚫ 8 月以來布蘭特原油大致位於每桶 85-95 美元區間，預期年底前仍將對
通膨帶來額外壓力，後續須關注能源漲價可能會透過運輸、物流等成本
管道傳導至核心商品及服務價格，進而提高核心通膨黏性。 
日 
本 
製造業產出穩健成長，消費動能仍具韌性

短評 
⚫ 7 月零售與工業生產同步改善，顯示內需及製造業動能均維持穩健。其
中 AI 相關需求持續帶動製造業擴張；消費支出則受耐久財及非耐久財
需求回升支撐，短期景氣仍具韌性。 
中 
國 
8 月景氣略為回穩，但前景仍有較多不利因素 
經濟

51.4。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ G20 峰會將中國龐大貿易順差視為加劇全球經濟失衡的重要因素之
一，國際社會要求中國擴大內需及調整經濟結構的壓力持續升高。 
短評 
⚫ 展望未來，中國出口環境仍面臨國際貿易保護主義升溫的壓力影響，9
月 24 日川習會若會談結果正向，將有助降低出口面臨之政策風險。

一、 美國—就業回穩支撐景氣韌性，惟通膨壓力與貿易逆差擴大增添政策不確
定性 
2026 年 8 月美國就業市場呈現回穩跡象，非農就業人數較上月增加 16.2 萬
人，不僅高於上月修正後的 2.1 萬人，也明顯優於過去 12 個月平均每月增加 3.1
萬人的水準；失業率則維持於 4.1%，失業人數約 703.1 萬人，顯示勞動市場整體
尚未明顯轉弱。從產業表現來看，新增就業主要集中於餐飲服務業增加 5.9 萬人
及地方政府教育部門增加 4.2 萬人 ， 製造業亦增加1.6 萬人 ， 持續支撐就業成長 ；
醫療保健業增加 1.3 萬人，雖維持擴張，但增幅低於過去一年平均水準。另一方
面 ， 資訊業減少2.3 萬人 ， 為主要拖累來源 ， 反映部分產業仍處於人力調整階段 。
勞動市場結構方面，勞動參與率回升至 61.6%，較前月改善，但仍較今年 1 月低
0.5 個百分點；就業人口比率則上升至 59.1%。薪資與工時方面，民營部門平均每
週工時升至 34.4 小時，平均時薪增至 37.75 美元，月增 0.3%、年增 3.1%，薪資
成長保持溫和。整體而言，8 月就業數據顯示美國勞動市場在前月疲弱後回穩，
且前兩個月數據上修，反映就業市場仍具韌性。強勁就業報告公布後，美債殖利
率走升，市場對聯準會 9 月維持偏緊政策的預期升溫，升息可能性亦有所提高。 
2026 年 7 月美國職位空缺數為 727.1 萬個，較上月增加 8.9 萬個，職缺率由
4.3%升至 4.4%；聘僱人數為 505.4 萬人，較上月減少 27.8 萬人，聘僱率由 3.4%
降至 3.2%；總離職人數為 507.2 萬人，較上月減少 26.5 萬人，離職率亦由 3.4%
降至 3.2%，顯示勞動市場流動性有所降溫。就離職結構觀察，主動離職人數為
305.6 萬人，較上月減少 15.7 萬人；裁員與解僱人數為 166.6 萬人，較上月減少
11.9 萬人 。 產業別方面 ， 職位空缺增加主要集中於耐久財製造業(增加 7.6 萬個)；
聘僱人數則以專業及商業服務業減少 18.8 萬人最為明顯 ； 主動離職人數以住宿及
餐飲服務業減少 8.6 萬人最多。整體而言，7 月美國職缺小幅增加，顯示企業用
人需求仍具支撐，惟聘僱及離職規模同步縮減，反映勞動市場流動性趨緩；加以
主動離職率及裁員率雙雙下降，顯示勞工轉職意願與企業人力調整均趨於審慎，
勞動市場供需大致維持平衡。

上月的 55.6 下降 1.0 點，惟已連續第八個月維持在 50 以上擴張區間，顯示美國
製造業景氣持續擴張。細項觀察，新訂單指數由56.7 降至 53.7，雖連續第八個月
處於擴張區間，但需求動能較前月放緩；生產指數由58.5 微降至 58.3，連續第十
個月擴張，產出活動維持穩健；就業指數由52.8 降至 51.2，仍處擴張區間，反映
企業聘僱需求持續增加。供應商交貨指數由58.9 升至 59.3，供應商交貨時間持續
延長；庫存指數則由 51.2 降至 50.6，企業仍持續累積庫存，但備貨動能較前月趨
緩。此外，價格指數維持71.1 不變，原物料價格仍處相對高檔；新出口訂單指數
由 53.0 微升至 53.2， 連續第二個月維持擴張 ； 惟未完成訂單指數由55.0 降至 51.8，
進口指數亦由 55.7 降至 52.5，顯示部分需求與投入動能有所降溫。整體而言，8
月美國製造業活動雖仍維持擴張，但擴張力道較前月略有減弱，新訂單、就業、
未完成訂單及進口等指標同步回落，反映需求成長動能趨緩；另一方面，供應商
交貨時間持續延長、原物料價格維持高檔，加以中東地緣政治衝突、關稅政策及
供應鏈不確定性仍存，可能持續對製造業活動形成壓力。 
2026 年 8 月美國 ISM 服務業採購經理人指數(Services PMI) 升至 55.4，較上
月 54.1 上升 1.3 個百分點，連續第26 個月維持在 50 以上擴張區間，且高於近12
個月平均值 53.7，顯示美國服務業景氣持續穩健擴張。商業活動指數由59.1 升至
61.7，創 2022 年 11 月以來最高水準；新訂單指數由 57.2 升至 60.9，連續 15 個
月維持擴張，創 2023 年 2 月以來新高，顯示需求動能進一步增強。勞動市場方
面，雇傭指數由 47.4 微升至 47.8，但已連續兩個月處於收縮區間，反映企業聘僱
態度仍偏審慎。價格指數由 70.3 升至 72.6，創 2022 年 8 月以來最高水準，顯示
通膨與成本壓力依舊偏高。供應鏈方面，供應商交期指數由52.8 降至 51.3，雖連
續 21 個月高於 50，但已連續第四個月改善；庫存指數則由 51.4 升至 56.7，顯示
企業備貨意願增強。此外，新出口訂單指數及進口指數均升至 56.3，反映外部需
求與採購活動同步回溫。整體而言，8 月美國服務業景氣擴張力道進一步增強，
商業活動與新訂單表現亮眼，惟就業指數仍低於榮枯線，加上企業持續關注關稅
政策及中東衝突對供應鏈與成本的影響，後續仍須觀察通膨與勞動市場發展。 
2026 年 7 月美國商品及服務貿易逆差擴大至 886 億美元 ， 較上月增加174 億
美元，增幅 24.4%。當月出口 3,107 億美元，較上月減少 66 億美元(-2.1%)；進口
3,993 億美元，較上月增加 108 億美元(+2.8%)。其中，商品貿易逆差擴大至1,196

下滑主要受工業用品及原材料出口減少 87 億美元影響，其中原油及非貨幣黃金

8 月 30 日美軍以伊朗革命衛隊(IRGC)疑似準備於荷姆茲海峽部署水雷 、 威脅
商業航運安全為由，對伊朗拉拉克島(Larak Island) 火箭發射設施發動精準打擊，
為美方逾一個月來首度對伊朗本土目標採取軍事行動。伊朗隨後以飛彈及無人機
攻擊約旦與阿聯酋境內美軍相關設施作為回應，顯示衝突已由雙邊軍事對抗逐步
向區域層面擴散。至 9 月 5 日美國中央司令部(CENTCOM)表示，因伊朗革命衛
隊向兩艘美國海軍艦艇發射彈道飛彈，美軍已對 3 艘伊朗油輪發動攻擊，其中一
艘位於伊朗重要石油出口樞紐哈爾克島(Kharg Island)外海。美方稱未造成人員傷
亡，惟伊朗半官方媒體指出，哈爾克島錨地附近油輪遭 4 枚美軍飛彈擊中，船員
已展開撤離。由於哈爾克島為伊朗最重要的原油出口據點，戰前約九成原油出口
均經由該地，加上荷姆茲海峽為全球關鍵能源運輸通道，本次衝突已明顯升高市
場對油輪航行安全、原油供應穩定性及運輸成本上揚的憂慮，帶動布蘭特原油期
貨於 9 月 4 日收報每桶 96.28 美元，創 7 月 24 日以來新高。整體而言，美伊衝突
已由原先針對軍事設施的交鋒，進一步延伸至能源運輸與油輪目標，若後續報復
與反制行動持續升級，恐加劇中東原油供應中斷風險，推升全球能源價格與通膨
壓力，並提高市場對美國經濟前景及聯準會貨幣政策走向的不確定性。 
二、歐元區—通膨壓力回升，核心通膨走向值得關注 
 歐元區 7 月失業率持平 6.4%，失業人數持平上月水準。其中德國失業人數增
加 1.1 萬人，失業率持平為 4%；法國、義大利失業人數皆下降 1 萬人，失業率分
別持平為 8.3%、5.8%； 西班牙失業人數持平上月 ， 惟因部分勞動力退出勞動市場 ，
使失業率略降為 10.1%。整體而言，失業率維持在 6.3%-6.4%的低點區間波動，
且 8 月 PMI 的就業指數也有所增溫， 顯示勞動市場的韌性可望延續， 並支持消費
需求表現。 
 歐元區 8 月 HICP 通膨上升 0.4 個百分點至 3.3%，其中因燃料價格上揚，能
源通膨上升 4 個百分點至 14.3%，食品通膨則穩定於 1.2%，迄今能源及化肥價格

預期年底前仍將對通膨帶來額外壓力，後續須關注能源漲價可能會透過運輸、物
流等成本管道傳導至核心商品及服務價格，進而提高核心通膨黏性。 
三、日本—製造業產出穩健成長，消費動能仍具韌性 
7 月零售月增 2.4%，部份反映 6 月受梅雨及豪雨天氣影響後的回升，顯示消
費動能仍具韌性。其中耐久財消費持續受到政策及汰舊換新需求支撐，家電銷售
在 6 月大幅下滑後明顯回升 ， 主要受空調等產品需求帶動 ； 汽車銷售額大致持平，
顯示政府自 4 月推動的稅務優惠措施效果尚未消退。另，非耐久財消費亦呈現全
面改善，服裝銷售受炎熱天氣帶動而大幅回升，一般商品銷售則反映國內消費及
觀光需求同步增強，食品銷售亦維持成長， 惟部分增幅可能並非實際需求增加，
若未來食品通膨進一步升溫，恐壓縮家庭實質購買力及消費信心，進而對後續非
耐久財支出及消費動能形成壓力。 
7 月工業生產月增 0.1%，主要係人工智慧相關需求帶動，科技與非科技產業
產出同步增加，其中半導體製造設備、電子零組件及化學工業表現尤為亮眼。出
貨月增 2.2%，高於生產增幅，而庫存僅增 0.5%、庫存率下降 1.7%，顯示產品去
化情況良好，整體供需維持穩健。展望未來，企業預估 8 月工業生產將大幅成長
6.4%，雖 9 月可能略為回落，但PMI 等前瞻指標仍顯示製造業活動持續擴張，預
計第三季製造業產出可望維持穩健成長。

8 月官方製造業 PMI 回升 0.6 點至 49.8，連續兩個月處於微幅衰退區間，新

供應鏈交期指數上升至 50.1、投入物價指數回升至 56.6，反映交期拉長及成本加
速上漲，使業者營運承壓；產出物價指數回升 2.6 點至 50.4，售價調整速度依然

展望未來，G20 峰會將中國龐大貿易順差視為加劇全球經濟失衡的重要因素
之一，國際社會要求中國擴大內需及調整經濟結構的壓力持續升高。此外，中國
仍面臨美國 301 關稅調查、歐盟擴大貿易保護措施等，出口環境仍具不確定性。
未來將關注 9 月 24 日川習會，預計雙方將就關稅措施、出口管制、AI 及先進科
技管制等議題進行協商，若會談結果正向，將有助緩解中美貿易摩擦，降低出口
面臨之政策風險。 
五、印度及東南亞—印度經濟保持強勁、馬來西亞央行利率不變 
印度: 2026 年第二季(2026/27 財年第一季)經濟成長率 （GDP） 年增7.8%，持平於
上季，仍為二十國集團（G20）成長最快國家，成長最快行業依序為:金融服務業
12.1%、製造業 9.2%、公用事業 8.9%、貿易/旅館/運輸/通訊業 8.5%；不過，農業
產出僅增長 3.6%，採礦及採石業則收縮 2.4%。儘管上半年經濟成長表現強勁，
製造業、消費和信貸均展現韌性；不過，油價上漲、印度盧比承壓以及季風降雨
不足等風險持續積累 ， 並透過貿易逆差 、 通膨和企業成本等管道向實體經濟傳導 ，
為經濟前景增添不確定性。 
東南亞:8 月 S&P Global 東協 （ASEAN） 製造業採購經理人指數(PMI)較上月小幅
回落 0.5 點至 52.3，仍保持穩健擴張態勢，其中，新訂單以接近歷史高點的速度
增加，生產活動雖較前月放緩，仍保持穩健；採購活動持續擴張，但聘僱與原物
料庫存小幅下滑，顯示廠商在人力與庫存建置仍偏謹慎，未完成訂單持平前月，
顯示產能壓力依然存在。價格方面，投入成本與產品售價均降至六個月低點，通

### Page 10

本報告係根據研究資料及各項可靠數據作成，兆豐銀行保留報告內容所有權利，未經本行許可，請勿引
用相關內容。

9

膨壓力進一步緩和 ， 供應商交貨時間延遲情況較前月減輕 ； 企業信心進一步增強 ，
未來一年產出預期指數達近四年高點。按個別國家來看，8 月除印尼(49.8)自擴張
轉為收縮外 ， 馬來西亞(50.2)、 泰國(53.8)較上月下滑 ， 新加坡(59.4)、 菲律賓(54.9)、
越南(53.3)均加速擴張。整體而言，在需求穩健、供應鏈改善及價格壓力減輕下，
東協國家製造業可望保持穩健擴張態勢，惟美伊衝突的不確定性仍存，持續推升
通膨預期並干擾供應鏈，同時牽動全球資金流動，仍須留意其對生產及企業獲利
的影響。 
馬來西亞 ：馬來西亞央行 9 月貨幣政策會議 ， 如預期維持基準利率 （OPR）於2.75%
不變。央行指出，儘管全球經濟情勢存在不確定性，得益於強勁的出口、穩健的
就業與內需 ，2026 年上半年經濟成長率(GDP)達 5.7%， 預計2026 年全年可達 5%，
而良好基本面將使 2027 年的經濟成長保持韌性。通膨方面，2026 年前七個月，
平均整體通膨率和核心通膨率分別為 1.8%和 2%，儘管受中東緊張局勢影響，全
球大宗商品價格上漲，但預計物價壓力仍將保持在可控範圍內。整體而言，受益
於全球經濟前景的改善、科技相關出口需求強勁及旅遊業活動增加，為經濟成長
帶來上行潛力，惟地緣政治緊張局勢持續、全球金融環境趨緊以及對金融市場估
值過高的擔憂，仍帶來下行風險。目前的貨幣政策立場與物價持續穩定和經濟可
持續成長的前景相符 ， 未來將密切關注國際局勢變化 、 成本壓力和國內需求狀況 ，
彈性調整政策。

長榮 (2603 TT) 
2026 獲利亮眼且擁高現金殖利率

永豐觀點 
2026 年獲利逆勢上揚且淡季運價緩跌有撐，擁8%現金殖利率。觀察12 月巴拿馬
運河水位。 
 投資評價與建議 
維持買進：(1) 美洲持續暢旺到明年春節，亞洲進入旺季，運價淡季相對有撐，(2) 
2027 年新增運力被碼頭作業能量不易擴充而抵銷，實質有效運力可能低於市場預
期，(3) 高現金殖利率的保護，下檔有撐，維持買進，目標價由 256 元(0.9X2026 
BVPS)上調到 278 元(0.85X2027BVPS)。

ESG 評析 
長榮企業永續評鑑整體為 A 等，於 SASB 產業永續議題與跨產業五大永續面向平
均得分分別為 A 等與 A 等。

長榮為全球第 7 大海運業者：長榮海運是全球第 7 大航商，6 月底集團運力 204
萬 TEU，242 艘船舶，市佔率 5.9%，2025 年底運力 195.6 萬 TEU(+11%YoY)，
2026-2027 運力分別為 207.1 萬 TEU(+5.9%YoY)和 241 萬 TEU(+15.9%YoY)，
待交船 72 艘約 93.5 萬 TEU。1H26 年運送 545 萬 TEU(+4.2%YoY)，平均運費
1034 美元/TEU(-7%YoY)，2025 年航線別美洲線佔 39%，歐洲線佔 28%、亞洲
線佔 18%，其他佔 15%。 
3Q 運價強勢 ，4Q 運價相對有撐 ：1H26 美洲線艙位成長 0.96%， 貨量成長5.1%，
歐洲線艙位成長 7.5%，貨量成長 12.2%。地緣政治風險導致供應鏈不穩旺季提前
運送而上漲， 亞洲重要港口碼頭使用率偏高， 天候因素容易導致碼頭作業效率低造
成塞港與巴拿馬運河水位降低等均影響有效運力，支撐美東線運價重回 1 萬美元
/FEU 關卡，10/8 巴拿馬運河水位 85.2 英尺(-1.93%YoY)較 9 月中略有回升，但
11 月底水位雖回升到 85.9 英尺但低於去年同期 3.4%，航商預期美洲線貨量持續
暢旺到農曆春節，觀察 2Q27 美國是否啟動升息循環影響貨量。

2026 年供過增長率最低的一年：Alphaliner 預估 2026 需求成長 2.5%， 供給成長
率 4.7%，2026 年船舶供給成長率是近幾年最低的，目前貨櫃船在手訂單1400 萬
TEU，未來三年面臨新船大量下水壓力，然而公司認為受到地緣政治影響船期不穩
定性高、碼頭使用率偏高易受突發事件導致塞港、2028 年美西港口工人合約到期
等均影響有效運力，即使紅海復航採逐班逐航線，影響也會被抵銷，因此2027 年
的名目供給能否 100%轉為實質運力有待考驗 。 目前全球運力因為碼頭作業能量受
限、氣候因素、繞道、降速等影響 11%運力。 
美國零售進口 2026 年預計成長 1%：根據美國零售協會預估，2026 年 1-7 月貨
櫃進口 14.97 百萬 TEU(+0.3%YoY) ， 9 月進口量達到高峰 2.31 百萬
TEU(+10%YoY)，10 月開始下滑，但仍保持年成長，2026 年預估進口 25.7 百萬
TEU(+1%YoY)。

燃油成本的影響 ：新加坡船用燃油 3Q 月平均 574 美元/噸(-7%QoQ%+44%YoY)，
低硫油價格 3Q 月平均 798 美元/噸(+3%QoQ%,+58%YoY)，長榮安裝脫硫塔的
船舶超過 9 成，船齡相對年輕省油，2025 年燃油成本佔 21%，1H26 佔 22%，
2Q 反應油價上漲會提升。長榮 2025 年平均用油價格 485 美元/噸(-8.1%YoY)，
1-8M26 用油價格 581 美元/噸(+16.7%YoY)，1-8 月燃油成本提升到 23%。 
現金殖利率 ：預估明後年配發現金股利 20 元和 18 元 ， 現金殖利率為8.6%和 7.7%，
長線受高股息 ETF 的青睞。 
預估 3Q26 稅後獲利 413 億元(+90%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 19.08 元：3Q 旺季運價
亮眼 ， 營收1468 億元(+51%YoY)符合預期 ， 預估稅後獲利413 億元(+90%YoY)，
稅後 EPS 為 19.08 元。公司目前北美訂艙量到 11 月都相當樂觀，4Q 預估營收
1320 億元(+54%YoY)，稅後獲利 264 億元(+210%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 12.20 元。 
財務預估 ： 預估 2026 年營收 4704 億元 (+24%YoY)，稅後獲利 921 億元
(+34%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 42.52 元，每股淨值 290.4 元。2027 年雖然紅海逐漸
復航，但碼頭作業彈性低易塞港抵銷運力恢復，預估營收4666 億元(-0.8%YoY)，
稅後獲利 792 億元(-14%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 36.56 元，每股淨值 326.9 元。

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
We keep our 3Q26 iPhone build estimate unchanged at 54M units (+4% q/q, -2%
y/y). But we have made adjustments to new iPhone 18 builds by lowering iPhone
Duo by 0.5M units to 0.5M, while raising iPhone 18 Pro Max by 0.5M units to 8.5M.

For 4Q, we lower our iPhone build estimate by 1M units to 72M units (+33% q/q,
-5% y/y), reflecting lower legacy iPhone models (iPhone 16/17e). Total new iPhone
18 model family units remains at 71M units (-21% y/y) in 2H26, with one less SKU
launched compared to last year. We continue to expect 7M units of iPhone Duo
builds in 2H26. With iPhone Duo sales starting soon, we will continue to monitor
ramp-up progress for any upside or downside to builds. Our revised full-year iPhone

build forecast implies CY26 shipment at 234M units (+3% y/y).
Our 3Q26 iPad build forecast is lowered by 0.5M units to 11.5M units (-12% q/q, -
18% y/y). We lowered 3Q entry level iPad builds due to  pull-forward demand in 1H
and the impact of rising prices. We also lower our 4Q iPad build forecast by 0.7M
units to 10.3M units (-10% q/q, -26% y/y). Our revised full-year iPad build forecast
implies CY26 shipments at 46.8M units (-14% y/y).

Key Takeaways
3Q26e iPhone build unchanged at 54M units (+4% q/q, -2% y/y).
4Q26e iPhone build lowered slightly to 72M units (+33% q/q, -5% y/y).
3Q26e iPad build lowered slightly to 11.5M units (-12% q/q, -18% y/y).
4Q26e iPad build lowered to 10.3M units (-10% q/q, -26% y/y).

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Key Metrics      
* For full definitions of iQmethod SM measures, see page 6.

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
TSMC was founded in 1987 and is listed in Taiwan 
(2330TT) and the US (TSM/NYS).TSMC is the largest and 
global leader in integrated circuit (IC) manufacturing. As a 
build-to-order foundry, it provides a wide range of value-
add activities: IC manufacturing, mask-making, IC design 
services, turnkey solutions, and process development. We 
attribute its success to its proven, winning business model, 
unparalleled scale advantage, optimized execution, and 
technology scope and depth. 5 shares = 1 ADR.

Investment Rationale 
The ongoing semi content growth in mobile, rise of artificial 
intelligence (AI), and proliferation of Internet of Things (IoT) 
should result in sustainable upside in aggregate computing 
power globally. TSMC, as the leading contract manufacturer 
of semiconductor chips, is in a good position to capitalize. 
We are structurally positive on TSMC and expect it to 
sustain over 15% growth with rising FCF from 2026 
onwards.

### Page 4

Price objective basis & risk 
Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMWF / TSM) 
Our PO for TSMC is NT$3,260 per share (US$620 per ADR), based on 20x 2027E P/E. 
TSMC's valuation has traded in the range of 10-26x PE from 2017. 20x is in the mid to 
high end of the historical range, above the average P/E of 16x. We think a PE above the 
average is justified given TSMC's enhanced industry position and mid-to-high 50% 5-
year CAGR in AI should underpin a rerating

Downside risks to our price objective are: (1) greater-than-expected slowdown in global 
smartphone/consumer electronics demand, (2) Intel's potential insourcing strategy and 
ambitions in foundry service, (3) execution risks on advanced nodes, and (4) higher than 
expected tariffs.

壹、投 資 建 議 
重點摘要： 
 統新 2Q26 營收為 2.08 億元，+34.7%QoQ、+82.4%YoY，毛利率
躍升至 56.20%，稅後淨利 0.86 億元，單季稅後 EPS+2.22 元。
2Q26 受惠生產效率提升、產能滿載毛利率躍升。 
 AI 伺服器帶動統新的 DWDM 產品延伸至 AI 資料中心 DCI 主要
骨幹濾片，光纖波段分離與光纖傳輸頻寬增加，會需要統新薄
膜濾光片作為訊號傳輸強化。除 Scale-Across 長距離骨幹，分
散式 AI 數據中心，專門做長距離光通訊傳輸 DWDM 市場，
2026~2031 年每年維持 31%年複合成長。 
 1.6T 光纖收發模組 ， 未來 2 年呈倍數增長 。 1.6T 光纖收發模組，
在後端 PD(接收端)改採 200G PD，會用到 8 片統新之薄膜濾光
片。

一. 統新 2Q26 營收與財報優於市場預期，3Q26 續強 
統新 2Q26 營收為 2.08 億元，+34.7%QoQ、+82.4%YoY，毛利率躍
升至 56.20%，稅後淨利 0.86 億元，單季稅後 EPS+2.22 元。主要受
惠於 AI 資料中心互連需求，使得統新產線維持滿載。展望 3Q26
預估統新營收為 2.42 億元，+16.35%QoQ，毛利率 56.58%，稅後淨
利 1.06 億元，單季稅後 EPS+2.76 元。統新預計產能增加 30%，以
因應客戶需求。

二. 2026 年 DWDM 隨 AI 骨幹網絡建置，濾片量增價漲 
統新高密度波長多工器( D W D M，D e n s e  W a v e l e n g t h  D i v i s i o n  
Multiplexing)，至少可傳輸 16 種不同顏色的光纖，最高可傳輸 128
種不同顏色之光纖，同一條主幹網路上，可以傳輸的頻寬因此加
大，相當具有傳輸效率。DWDM 於發送端將不同波長的光信號組
合起來(覆用, MUX)，並耦合到光纜線路上的同一根光纖中，進行
傳輸，在接收端又將多個波長的光信號分開(解覆用, DEMUX)，並
作進一步處理，恢復出原波長信號後送入不同的終端接收器。統
新的 DWDM 產品延伸至 AI 資料中心 DCI 主要骨幹濾片，波段分
離 ( S -B a n d、C - B a n d 傳 輸 核 心、L -B a nd 容 量 擴 充、S -B a nd 未 來 預 留、
U-Band 8nm 用於 OTDR 光纖監控偵測主幹網路是否具有斷點或需
要救援)，統新以單通道 100GHz 去細分 DWDM 濾光片，統新推出
新品，50GHz 間隔濾光片，支援的通道數量提升至 96Channels。

目標價位：375.00 元

三.  AI 伺服器高速傳輸，帶動光通訊主被動元件量增 
Scale-Across 長距離骨幹，分散式 AI 數據中心，專門做長距離光
通訊傳輸 DWDM 市場，2026~2031 年，DWDM 市場每年維持 31%
年 複合 成長。此 外，受惠 AI 伺服 器傳 輸速 率提升，如：Broadcom、
Marvell 等國際晶片廠商，於 2026 年起已推出 800G /1.6T Switch 晶
片組，等同於 AI 伺服器後端 Ports 支援的光纖通訊傳輸會一併升
級至 800G 與 1.6T 傳輸速率。Yole 報告指出，1.6T 光纖收發模組
出貨量，2026 年/2027 年/2028 年預估量 15~20M/55~60M/85~100M，
1.6T 光纖收發模組 ， 未來 2 年內呈倍數增長 。 1.6T 光纖收發模組，
在後端 PD(接收端)改採 200G PD ， 會用到 8 片統新之薄膜濾光片。
在薄膜濾光片鍍膜層數 ， Scale-Across 與 NPO 所需薄膜濾光片層數
約 150L~200L，未來發展至 CPO 時鍍膜層數會再增加 8L，統新為
此預計多準備 10 條產線預計開發 250L+規格，予台系知名晶圓代
工廠商。統新指出，Scale-Out 濾片鍍膜代工量增加，是替中系系
統廠代工(層數約 100~150L)。Scale-Across 濾片鍍膜層數會大於
200L，甚至高達 250L，目前訂單能見度高，涵蓋 1.)同業產能難以
支應，Cohr 交給統新、2.)中系系統廠商加單、3.)台系網通廠商於
低軌衛星鍍膜(LEO 非光學鍍膜) 。統新毛利率之所以跳升，是先前
客戶給統新的試產量僅 20~30 片，2Q26 起量產量增至 K 級別，出
貨量增加、規模經濟效應之下，單月營收損平點(評估為 0.4~0.5
億元)，2Q26 起月營收顯著跳升至 0.6~0.7 億元。預期此營運動能
至少能見度到 4Q26，但由於 DWDM 交期延長至 6~8 個月，預估
2027 年產業供需偏向緊俏。

四.投資建議：買進，目標價 375 元 
統新競爭優勢在於其生產高品質高效率之薄膜濾光片篩選特定輻
射波段的光學元件，能篩選光的強度、改變光譜成分或限定震動
截面，藉由 CWDM/DWDM 技術使相同數據集中於同一根光纖中 
傳輸，增大傳輸容量進而提高光纖的使用效率，生產良率逐步提
升且具佔有一定之市場地位。預估統新 2026 年營收 8.56 億元，
+75.77%YoY，毛利率 53.43%，稅後淨利 3.32 億元，稅後 EPS 為
+8.63 元。展望 2027 年，隨產能積極擴充之下，預估統新年營收
為 17.02 億元，+98.83%YoY ，毛利率預估為 54.85% ，稅後淨利 5.57
億元，稅後 EPS+14.47 元。考量基礎建設帶動 DWDM 規格升級，
AI 伺服器傳輸速率提升因應 1.6T 傳輸薄膜濾光片用量同步增長，
給予統新投資建議買進，目標價 375 元(26.00 X2027 EPS)。

一.公司簡介

統新(6426)為生產精密光學元件，以及高密度分波多工器(DWDM)
關鍵元件─薄膜濾光片(TFF)之專業廠商。各產品線營收占比為主
動光學元件佔比低於 15%、被動光學元件：80~85%、光學鍍膜元
件與代工低於 10%。主要產品 DWDM 濾光片 (Dense Wavelength 
Division Multiplexer，即為高密度波長多工分工器)技術可以大幅提
高光纖通訊系統之頻寬，成為光纖通訊系統的市場中關注的焦
點。統新國內主要競爭對手為東典光電科技(股)，國外廠商包含
II-IV Cohernet(美國)、Iridian Technologies(加拿大)等廠商投入相關
產品研發及生產。

圖一、光通訊元件

資料來源：公司、宏遠投顧整理

二.26 年營運概況及 26 年展望 
1.統新 DWDM 技術層層堆疊良率優異  
統新主要產品為薄膜濾光片即為篩選特定輻射波段的光學元件，
能篩選光的強度、改變光譜成分或限定震動截面，藉由不同光線
原理而選擇通過的光源(紅、藍、綠光) ，且藉由 CWDM/DWDM 技
術使相同數據集中於同一根光纖中傳輸，增大傳輸容量進而提高
光纖的使用效率。

2.因應 AI 資料中心基建商機，薄膜濾光片產品走向更高規  
回顧 5G 時期的薄膜濾光片，支援頻段增加，切割更細，中心波

長 1267.5nm、1274.5nm 切割更為細窄，至 1374.5nm 從過往支援
波長 6 組延伸至 12 組 ， 每一道光波段間距縮短至 3.5nm 甚至更
短，薄膜濾波濾光片用量增加，設計難度提升。隨著支援頻段愈
切分愈細，波段之間間距愈來愈短，需要用的薄膜濾波片數量倍
增，且每一片薄膜濾波片設計難度提升，包含設計層數 200 層以
上以及過濾效果較佳，而統新於濾波片設計良率優於競爭同業，
可望取得一定市場地位。

圖二、DWDM 系列產品設計隨過濾頻段複雜，設計難度提升

Issue & News 
SK하이닉스  
(000660) 
 소음보다 방향성에 
류형근

hyungkeun.ryu@daishin.com

투자의견 BUY  
매수, 유지 
6개월 목표주가 3,200,000 
유지  
현재주가 
(26.09.23) 
1,862,000 
반도체업종

◼ 최근 시장은 2가지 Risk를 제기. 다만, 과도한 우려는 기우. 
◼ 현 주가는 보다 강인해지는 Fundamental의 가치를 미반영한 수준. 
◼ 비중 확대 전략 유효. 목표주가와 매수의견 유지.

투자전략: 업황을 대변하지 못하고 있는 주가 
목표주가와 매수의견 유지. 현 주가는 업황의 강세를 충분히 반영하지 못하
고 있다고 생각. 1) 메모리반도체 사이클의 추가 확장 (구매 경쟁 심화에 따른 
단기 가격 상승 폭 확대, 기대치를 상회할 4Q26 DRAM 판매량), 2) 3Q26 실
적발표에서 보다 구체화될 주주환원 정책의 방향성에 보다 집중할 필요. 
시장에서 제기하는 Risk가 있다면, 2가지. 1) 주요 고객사향 HBM4 품질 이슈, 
2) Solidigm IPO와 해외 공장 투자에 따른 경계심리 확대가 그 대상. 당사는 
해당 Risk가 Fundamental을 저해할 것으로 보고 있지 않으며, 단기 노이즈보
다는 사이클 확장의 선순환에 보다 주목할 필요가 있다고 생각. 
1. HBM4 품질 이슈, 과도한 우려는 기우 
3Q26부터 HBM4 출하가 본격화될 것이라는 기존 입장을 유지. HBM4 비중
은 3Q26 전체 판매량의 40%, 2027년 연간 50% 중반까지 상승할 것. 
일각에서는 주요 고객사향 HBM4 품질 이슈로 사업 성과가 기대치를 하회할 
수 있다는 우려를 표출. 다만, 과도한 우려는 기우. 후공정 단에서 문제가 일
부 발생했을 수 있으나, 해결 불가능한 Risk라 보기 어렵고, 불량에 따른 배
상금을 논할 단계도 아니라는 판단. 
2. 해외 공장 투자, 실보다 득이 크다 
언론보도에 따르면, SK하이닉스는 해외 공장 투자와 Solidigm IPO를 추진 중. 
해당 결정이 현실화될 경우, 시장은 단기적으로 이를 부정적으로 받아들일 
가능성이 존재. 다만, 결과적으로, 실보다 득이 큰 선택이 될 것이라 생각. 
- 해외 투자가 현실화되더라도, 실질 생산 기여 시점은 2028년 이후일 가능
성이 높음. 미국의 건설 경기와 공장 구축의 세부 단계 등을 감안할 필요. 
- 해외 투자 결정이 관세 Risk를 축소시킬 수 있는 변화라는 점도 감안할 필
요. “불확실성 해소”는 Upcycle의 중반부에서 반도체 주식에 대한 좋은 투자
포인트가 되어왔고, 이번에도 다르지 않을 것이라는 판단.

Document 245: Allianz_2026-10-20_European defense one year on_ Money is not the problem, capacity (and delivery) are_1d7796ef

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-20_European defense one year on_ Money is not the problem, capacity (and delivery) are_1d7796ef.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 20
Status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• European defense one year on: bold commitments, but is delivery keeping pace? One year 
into Europe's landmark rearmament announcements: defense spending has increased, 
financial commitments have expanded (e.g. SAFE loans, +60% commitments in 2028-2034 EU 
draft budget ) and order books have reached record highs . But industrial investment and 
delivery have yet to catch up. Defense spending growth has been cut in half  (9% vs. above 
20% in 2024/2025) , and is  set to reach  2.6% of GDP by 2030  (3.1% of GDP by 2035 ), falling 
short of the EU 2030 target of 3% and the 2035 NATO target of 3.5% of GDP . This means a 
funding gap of EUR255bn and EUR575bn, respectively.  
• In equity markets, defense companies are facing a painful reality check but the hype is not 
over yet. European defense has lost its magic (+5% YTD but -8% maximum drawdown) after 
three consecutive years of strong performance (35 -60%), a clear reality check from investors 
as countries take diverging paths : Baltic  and Nordic countries, as well as  Germany and 
Poland are leading the effort while Italy and Spain are not using the available fiscal tools to 
boost spending. Capital has been mostly channeled into  equipment, rather than long-term 
infrastructure project s (0.11% of GDP  against NATO recommendations of 1.5% of GDP). In 
term of innovation, defense is lagging the AI and robotics conversion but rising interest from 
the private investment market (above EUR10bn vs. EUR2.6bn in 202 5) suggests that the 
innovation cycle still has strong funding leeway. 
• Money is not the constraint , conversion is. Governments have poured commitments into a 
sector where newcomers cannot quickly acquire what incumbents built over decades, so 
excess demand turns into production bottlenecks and postponed projects rather than output. 
Backlogs stand at more than five times revenue while the capex ratio has stalled near 5% . 
Diverging national interests , no supranational steer  and the absence of conditionality 
mandatory clauses in public financial support give the industry little reason to invest at a time 
when higher interest rates and material costs from the Iran conflict have cut m argins (from 
10% to 8%). The shortage of skilled labor adds to the pressure as firms compete for engineers 
with civilian sectors that pay more, and cannot quickly transition their workforce into new 
complex programs. The shortage is sharpest where Europe started late : drones, space and 
cybersecurity segments account for 26%, 16% and 13% of open defense vacancies. 
• The ongoing depletion of US interceptor stocks is another warning for Europe: external  
dependence is both a security risk and a capital -efficiency problem.  To fill urgent gaps, 
Europe buys from abroad, and the import ratio has risen from 20% in 2021 to 45% in 2025. 
The US’s share in European imports has fallen from 71% to 48% but it still supplies almost half, 
and aircraft and missiles make up 82% of US purchases, with few alternatives for air defense 
and long-range missiles. We estimate it could take up to five years to rebuild some US stocks; 
until then, US domestic demand will come first, and Europe will wait in the queue behind it. 
Dependence is also expensive: Missile import prices are up 116% on the 2019 -2021 average, 
so each euro buys less capacity than budgets assume.  
• The way forward: Two horizons but one single industrial roadmap. In the short term, Europe 
needs to close the gaps in long -range missiles, air defense and cyber, and redirect idle 
automotive capacity to defense. Furthermore, it needs to back this with grants for 
infrastructure (a 0.5-1% of GDP corridor, or EUR760bn to E UR1.4trn cumulative to 2035) and 
fiscal flexibility conditional on results. In the long term, Europe needs to build supranational 
reserves and invest in drones, space, AI, robotics and quantum. These would spread benefits 
across the economy and protect the export model. Success should be judged on new capacity, 
spillovers, modernization and reliance rate, not on the share of GDP alone.  
Guillaume Dejean 
Senior Sector advisor 
guillaume.dejean@allianz -
trade.com

Ano Kuhanathan

Allianz Research | 20 October 2026 
European defense one year on: Money is not the 
problem, capacity (and delivery) are

### Page 2

2

Rebuilding European defense: ambition meets reality 
One year after the Rearm Europe roadmap was unveiled, the question is no longer one of intent but means. Does 
Europe have the financial and material resources to match its rhetoric?  European defense spending is rising, but 
not fast enough. At an expected 2.3% of GDP in 2026, up from 2.2% in 2025, it is set to record a second consecutive 
year above the 2% threshold. Yet the headline masks a sharp deceleration: Annual growth in outlays is falling to 
about 9%, from more than 20% in 2024-2025, and will drift toward 4-7% by 2028-2030. This new 5-10% regime looks 
insufficient for to meet the ambitions of Rearm Europe. Based on our growth and inflation forecasts for 2026 -2027, 
and a moderate 1% growth/2% inflation path thereafter, spending is likely to climb  to only 2.6% of GDP by 2030, 
missing the 3% target. The shortfall is material: a cumulative EUR255bn funding gap over 2026 -2030, widening to 
EUR575bn if we consider NATO’s new 3.5% of GDP target for core military spending. 
Figures 1 & 2: European defense spending trajectory by 2030  / cumulative spending gap based on 3% and 3.5% 
GDP budget target (in EUR bn)

Sources: 
 European Defense Agency (EDA),  
Allianz Research  
Europe's rearmament challenge is increasingly about execution, not just ambition. At the current pace, doubling 
Europe's 2025 military equipment stock would take around five years, leaving little room for delays before the 2030 
deadline. Faster progress would require not only higher defense spending, but also a greater share of budgets 
devoted to equipment and infrastructure, alongside a stronger European industrial base. Redirecting procurement 
towards European manufacturers  by reducing the share of imports from 45% to 25% could help accelerate capacity 
expansion. Under the most optimi stic scenario, these combined measures could shorten the timeline from five to 
around three years. But Europe cannot supply itself and remains highly dependent on foreign partners to procure 
its military equipment. The defense spending allocation shows a 35-40% disbursement into new equipment (vs. 30-
35% on NATO standards)  and 0-5% into infrastructure construction/maintenance, which is quite strong by NATO 
standards but not as ambitious as promised.

Money is no longer the main constraint: capacity is 
Money is not Europe’s binding constraint . The draft 2028 -2034 EU budget lifts defense-related commitments to 
around EUR270bn, at least 60% above the roughly EUR170bn of 2021 -2027. That estimate excludes the European 
Investment Bank, which devoted 4 -5% of its annual envelope to defense in 2025 -2026, or EUR4 -5bn a year. 
Extrapolated over seven years, this adds EUR30-35bn and takes the total mark-up to about 80%. The signal matters 
as much as the amount. While national budgets face fiscal pressure, Brussels is locking in a multi-year commitment, 
with no sign of fading effort at the European level. The composition is shifting too: military funds, marginal in 2021 -
2027, become the largest block of the next envelope, a clear break with past practice. Off -budget instruments 
reinforce the message, and new tools modelled on current program s could follow if demand and delivery justify 
them. For industrials and investors, the financing architecture is in place and still expanding. The question is no 
longer whether capital exists, but whether it reaches the factories that must turn it into equ ipment.

### Page 4

4

SAFE loans illustrate the solution but also the problem. Off-budget and capped at EUR150bn, they should channel 
roughly EUR80bn into regional efforts if distribution is gradual and homogeneous. The reality is far from that. Only 
EUR12bn has been disbursed, about 8% of the total, against EUR131bn formally adopted. Poland, the largest 
beneficiary at EUR43.7bn, has drawn 15% of its envelope. France, Italy, Belgium and Spain have received nothing 
yet, and  Hungary’s EUR16.2bn is still pending. Industrials have long blamed red tape and lengthy validation 
procedures for construction delays and underperforming projects, and SAFE is repeating the pattern. The 
multiplicity of programs (EDIP, FED, AGILE, innovati on funds, EIB defense lines) compounds the issue: they do not 
connect to industrial segments and leave firms navigating parallel application processes. Participation is uneven as 
well, with several states slow to apply. Without faster execution, the money risks arriving after the equipment gap 
has widened, and after the political window that justified it has narrowed.  
Table 1: Safe procurement program allocation and utilization rate

Sources: 
 European Commission,  
Allianz Research

The European fiscal impulse tool was not used to its full potential. Europe’s fiscal loosening  leeway, authorizing 
18 members to run until extra deficits up to 1.5% of GDP for defense spending showed some results, but not as much 
as expected. Among positive results, it clearly helped push military spending back above NATO’s former 2%-of-GDP 
benchmark in 2025 for the first time , and keep the momentum up in  2026. Among the 18 eligible countries, 12 are 
expected to run deficits above 3% in 2026 ( 70%) and only eight of them recorded a double -digit growth of military 
spending over the 2025/2026 period compared with 2024 ahead of the launch of the tool . If some countries like 
Poland, Latvia, Estonia or even Germany are good examples of the positive externalities of the NEC on European 
defense ramp-up efforts, the overall picture is quite uneven at the European level. Indeed, a higher deficit is not the 
guarantee of stronger defense spending, as showed by  Belgium, Hungary and Slovakia , which carry high deficits 
but grew military budgets by only 4-5% in 2026. Italy, Spain and the Netherlands raised spending by a modest 5-8% 
and stayed close to Brussels’ 3% line. With interest rates high and markets volatile, France’s deficit being the 
cautionary example, that prudence is understandable. The NEC works, but it is still far from being used to its full 
potential. This clearly demonstrates that Europe is not running out of resources to rebuild its army but rather of will, 
at public or private level.

Procurement is also crowding out the long term . Spending favors short-term equipment gaps, while defense 
infrastructure stands at only 0.11% of GDP in 2026, a small share of regional investment. Quick buys beat durable 
capacity, which leaves the underlying production base no stronger. The undersubscription to SAFE loans is the 
sharpest evidence: the program did not attract enough demand, and EUR10 -20bn is likely to be reallocated to 
Ukraine. Europeans are not short of funding but of capacity to buy. Sourcing rules limit purchases outside Europe, 
yet domestic supply cannot absorb the orders either, so governments can neither buy abroad nor at home as much 
as they would like. Strict rules on foreign investment in sensitive defense assets further limit outside capital for new 
capacity, closing another route to faster expansion. The pattern points one way: Europe’s bottleneck is industrial, 
not financial. The next euro of ambition should go to factories, infrastructure and faster exe cution, not to larger 
envelopes. Otherwise Europe will keep announcing money it cannot spend.

### Page 6

Europe’s industrial conundrum: a record-high backlog did not translate into new investment. European defense 
has never had so much demand, yet its largest firms are not building the capacity to serve it. Order backlogs have 
hit record highs, at more than five times trailing revenue for the ten biggest listed groups, driven mostly by national 
governments. Revenue still expands at around +10% on a 12-month trailing basis, but the trend is flattening, which 
raises a question: has the peak already been reached? Capex points the same way. After the surge of 2023 -2024, 
the capex ratio has stabilized around 5%, a natural ceiling that matches the  lack of new impulse on sales. Orders 
are being booked faster than they can be converted into output. This is the industrial conundrum. The timing for 
investing could hardly be better, with full order books and political tailwinds. But two brakes are holding firms back: 
a monetization lag between orders and cash, and mild confidence in the durability of state commitment. Managers 
would rather move slowly than be left with overcapacity if budgets disappoint. The consequence is a self -inflicted 
bottleneck: the demand is there, but the supply response is rationed by caution.  
Figure 8 & 9: Revenue & investment trend of top 10 biggest defense firms in Europe* / Ammunition infrastructure

The d efense industry is adopting a defensive approach to cash management: securing margin s rather than 
chasing new revenue . Scaling up fast without disrupting current activity or inflating costs is harder than early 
optimism suggested, and the initial enthusiasm for capacity expansion has given way to caution as delivery under 
high-stakes pressure turns out to be tougher than p lanned. Visibility is the second brake. Future budgets and 
governance remain uncertain in a shifting political landscape, with the far -right push visible in several European 
countries adding doubt over the orientation and durability of defen se priorities. All these elements put together 
drive a weak incentive to invest aggressively  in expensive and long -term project s. Market conditions have also 
turned. Tensions in the Middle East have lifted energy and raw material costs, and the S&P GSCI industrial metals 
index is back near its 2022 highs, while higher borrowing costs make every risk more expensive. The margin picture  
shows the squeeze: net profit margins of the top European defen se firms peaked around 10% in 2025 and have 
slipped back to about 8%. In this environment, protecting margins is more rational than chasing new revenue. 
Backlogs are already full and the sector enjoys relative protection from foreign competition, so there is little need 
to take risks to win new orders. Defen se groups are therefore becoming defensive themselves, prioriti zing cash 
discipline over expansion. The outcome is an industry that has the orders but not the appetite, and a Europe whose 
rearmament will be paced by corporate prudence rather than by political ambition.

The undesirable effects of a backlog. Governments have poured commitments into a sector where newcomers 
cannot acquire in a few months what incumbents built over decades, so excess demand turns into production 
bottlenecks and postponed projects rather than output. The structure compounds the problem. Defense ministries 
have become the primary customer, competition is weak and contracts are concentrated on a handful of players. 
That is the captive market trap: a secured backlog, instead of lifting investment appetite, breeds complacency and 
decision inertia on new infrastructure. Rheinmetall is the case in point. With about 60% of its backlog coming from 
the Bundeswehr, it cut its revenue guidance in Q2 after removing a naval project it had committed to but could not 
deliver as it was far from its core ammunition business. Diversification is sound only when it is coherent and gradual. 
Markets have been unforgiving: the share has lost about 35% year to date, and market capitali zation fall en to 
roughly EUR 40 -45bn while forward EBIT growth has slipped from its 2026 peak of over 50% to below 50%. The 
sector that was once in the spotlight is now paying for the hype, with disappointing delivery feeding a market verdict 
that could weigh on the next round of investment.

### Page 8

Europe’s defense industry is running out of people before it runs out of orders.  We estimate the sector employs 
650,000-700,000 people, up 30-40% since 2021, while military spending has risen 75% and backlogs have more than 
doubled. The gap is widening. In our sample of leading firms, backlog growth per point of workforce growth was 
about 2 before 2025, rose to 3 in 2025 and reache d 4 in H1 2026, as EU rearmament program s and fiscal escape 
clauses poured money into order books. This is more than a capacity limit. Firms have little room to hire at scale: 
they compete for engineers with civilian sectors that pay more, and newcomers need years of training before they 
can deliver on complex program s. Expertise cannot be bought, only built, and in defense industrial lead times are 
long and some skills exist nowhere else. The shortage is sharpest where Europe started late. Drones and UAVs 
account for 26% of open defense vacancies, space 16%, intelligence and surveillance 13%, electronics 9%: segments 
in which Europe was largely absent until recently. Industry also risks overestimating what it can absorb. Money 
moves at political speed, learning curves at industrial speed. Scaling up with too few or poorly matched staff brings 
delays and failures, so the backlog will convert slowly.

Who is piloting the plane ? 
Europe’s defense effort is moving at two speeds. The 2025 -2026 comparison splits the continent into leaders 
(Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia and Poland ), which are heading toward defense spending at 4.5-5.5% of GDP in 2026 
and a pack of laggards, including France, Italy, Belgium, Czechia and Slovenia, which are still below 2.5%. Denmark 
and Sweden sit in between, above the diagonal. Geography sets the gradient first: Proximity to Russia determines 
the sense of emergency. But national equipment levels and domestic industrial strength matter as well. Frontline 
states are rebuilding from thin inventories, while larger Western economies feel less pressed and can lean on 
existing stocks and industrial bases. Politics reinforces the divide. Household surveys show strong appetite for 
intervention in Sweden, Estonia, Denmark, Poland and the UK, but little in France, Germany and Italy, where 
respondents also reject trading off ot her public spending for defense. Where popular support is thin or social 
cohesion fragile, governments hesitate to push ambition. The risk is that the largest economies, which hold the 
biggest industrial bases, set the slowest pace, leaving aggregate momentum dependent on a handful of s mall, 
frontline budgets. Europe cannot be defended by its Baltic fringe alone.

Europe’s biggest competitor is itself. It is quite straightforward to see that European nations do not ride in the same 
direction. A quick look at the  industrial project pipeline shows duplication rather than scale. Two rival next -
generation fighters are under way: the Global Combat Air Program ( GCAP) led by the trident UK, Italy, Japan and 
the French-led Air Combat Fighter (ACF), the successor to former French-German Future Combat Air System (FCAS) 
initiative whose failure this year  illustrates how  national interests can sink a supra -regional target. On land, four 
separate platforms (MGCS, Challenger 3, K2PL, A2CS) are built by four different coalitions. All are not European-
led as K2PL is a hybrid partnership between Poland and South Korea to replicate the flagship K2 tank used by the 
South Korean army . This sends a diverging signal : Europe can find partners other than the US for military 
procurement, but also it still needs foreign expertise to develop heavy equipment quickly  (three-to-five year 
timeline). For comparison, some joint projects, like the French-German Main Ground Combat System (MGCS), have 
an industrial lead of over 20 years, and the latter also faces industrial divergences between partners.  Behind what 
is said about the slow development of  FCAS and MGCS is a Franco-German leadership contest that is the cause of 
all this fragmentation. While two pilots are better than none, they do not share the same view in term s of defense: 
Paris pushes sovereignty and autonomous industrial capacity, while Berlin prioriti zes rapid re -equipment to be

effective if war comes. The current air defense pipeline shows the split. The German -led Sky Shield (ESSI) targets 
2027-2030, while the European Twister interceptor, driven by France, arrives in 2035 -2040. Both logics are 
defensible, but run in parallel they fragment demand, shorten production runs and raise uni t costs. The answer is 
not black or white. Europe needs short-term equipment to be credible, and buying off the shelf abroad is sometimes 
the fastest route, as K2PL with South Korea illustrates. But overreliance on external providers has a price: a weaker 
domestic industry, lost competitiveness and strategic dependence for decades. The workable middle path is to buy 
abroad for urgent gaps while anchoring long -cycle programs in genuine cooperation, with common requirements, 
shared leadership and pooled orders. Without that, Europe will spend more and get less.  
Industrial direction won’t come from a single voice but three. France, Germany and Italy account for about 80% of 
the European revenue among the world’s top 100 arms sellers, and for 67% of the EU’s military -related output in 
2026. The two measures point to different leaders. France dominates on corporate sales (37%), while Germany leads 
on production on its territory (34%). No major project can be defined, financed or delivered without all three, which 
makes their alignment the main lever for an investment trend. The setbacks on FCAS and MGCS did more than 
delay equipment: they fed doubts about whether Europe can ramp up production to meet its targets, and markets 
and managers read those signals closely. The trio should settle priorities, share leadership by project and commit 
to joint program with fixed schedules an d pooled funding. A credible Franco -German understanding would carry 
the most weight. It would show that cohesion is possible and give industry the visibility it needs to invest. Without it, 
the efforts of smaller countries will stay fragmented, whatever their budgets.

Beyond the rivalries, Europe’s military priori ty is  clearly the sky . Long -range missiles, air and missile defen se, 
drones and modernized fighters all answer the same shift in the nature of war. Modern conflict is moving away from 
massed land and naval engagements toward air strikes and drones aimed at tactical infrastructure, where 
disruption hurts the economy as much as the army. Ukraine made the point: Drones have become full combat tools, 
used for reconnaissance and targeting as well as for dropping munitions, at a fraction of the cost of traditional 
systems. The conflict involving Iran confirmed it, with data centers and power systems among the damaged assets. 
The implication is that deterrence now depends on striking deep, intercepting cheaply and protecting critical 
networks, rather than on heavy armor or large fleets. Europe’s list of priorities reflects that lesson. The consensus on 
what to buy is real, the remaining challenge is how to organize and fund it together.

Europe needs to bet on itself, not only for sovereign reasons 
External reliance has not disappeared but is changing shape. Foreign dependence has almost doubled since 
2022, from 24% to 45% of procurement, and the US share has dropped from 71% to 48%. But Washington is still the 
main supplier, and in the UK, the Nordics and the Baltics , it still supplies the large majority of external purchases. 
What is new is that buying abroad is starting to give way to partnering. German and Israeli primes already 
cooperate on missiles, loitering munitions and satellites, and a deal to turn a German car plant into an air defense 
site is taking shape. These are early signals, but they matter. Openness is the fastest way to meet short-term demand 
when domestic capacity is stretched , and real investment commitments on European soil turn a purchase into 
capacity. But openness is also hard to square with security stakes and sovereign risk: whoever owns the technology 
and the supply chain holds a lever over Europe’s defense. The condition is close monitoring: governance, ownership, 
access to critical know-how and exit options all need to be locked in upfront. This will not be a replicable template

### Page 12

12

for the whole sector but rather a tool for targeted, specific needs where Europe cannot wait for its own industry to 
catch up. 
Figures 19 & 20: Imports of arms, non-EU share & US share among non-EU imports / US arms import share in 
Europe, per region

Data as of 2025. Sources: SIPRI, Allianz Research  
Foreign dependence is also a price problem: inefficient capital allocation due to inflationary import costs.  Since 
2022, as Europe turned to non-European suppliers for arms and ammunition, import prices per kilogram have risen 
well beyond what a technology premium can justify. Compared with the 2019-2021 average, missiles now cost 116% 
more, more than double the pre-war level. Tanks and armored vehicles are up 83%, fighters 33% and helicopters 
18%. The pattern follows dependence. Where Europe has little or no domestic alternative, as with US interceptors 
and long-range missiles, suppliers have had room to pass premium margins on to buyers facing scarcity and urgent 
demand. Higher costs linked to AI expansion and the Middle East conflict play a role, but they do not explain the 
whole picture: prices have flattened this year for most categories despite renewed energy -driven inflation, which 
points to pricing power as much as input costs. The consequence is direct. Europe pays more for the same equipment, 
not because it is worth it but because it cannot wait. Each euro committed buys less capacity than budgets assume, 
so the real size of the rearmament is smaller than the headline.

The US ammunition crisis: an unpleasant but welcome wake -up call. The depletion of US missile stocks is a 
warning for Europe: a single supplier can fail it at any moment, by choice or by necessity. The Iran conflict has 
consumed interceptors at a pace production cannot match. Compared with pre -war levels, our reading of the data 
shows H1 2026 inventories down about 65% for Patriot, 45% for THAAD and 30% for Tomahawk. Washington’s 
answer is a rebuild: its FY27 request targets about 3,200 Patriot interceptors and 860 THAAD interceptors. But 
factories are the bottleneck, not budgets. Backlogs at US missile makers are growing at 28% a year against revenue 
growth of 9%, and orders now stand at about three times annual sales. On current ramp-up rates, we estimate four 
years to deliver the Patriot request and five for THAAD. Until then, US domestic demand will come first, and Europe 
will wait in the queue behind it. The US is not a last -resort partner, because it has no spare capacity to lend. The 
same weapons are now in short supply on both sides of the Atlantic, so Europe cannot count on  American stocks to 
cover its own air defense.

Europe’s defense industry needs three leaders, not one 
Rebuilding defense means thinking short-term with a long-term horizon state of mind. When defining European 
priorities in terms of procurement but also industrial investment, reducing US exports of arms and ammunitions to 
Europe would help to connect the dots . Aircraft account for 63% of EU imports from the US and missiles 19%, so 
these two segments make up over four -fifths of the total. Self -reliance has to be built there first. In the short term, 
Europe should widen its sourcing to cushion the post -Iran arbitrage in US production. On missiles, the options are 
narrow: Israel is the only credible supplier outside the US, since China and Russia are not an option. That choice 
should come with local production and investment commitments. On fighters, Europe can le an on its own 
technology and speed up upgrades, which makes cutting reliance on the US realistic even in the near term. On 
drones, Ukraine offers scale and unmatched combat experience, and Europe should plug into it through joint 
production and shared prac tice. Türkiye can support local manufacturing, and South Korea can help on armored 
vehicles and robotics as well.

Figure 25: breakdown of EU-27 imports from the US by military equipment type

Data as of 2025. Sources: SIPRI, Allianz Research  
Defense capacity should not be exclusively assessed in hardware . Software, IT and telecoms are overlooked, 
though new equipment carries a heavy digital load and produces vast amounts of data. Used well, that data is a 
performance driver. Used poorly, it is a vulnerability. Cybersecurity is the other missing layer: mod ern conflict is 
fought on networks as much as on the ground. It is no accident that, in the extra funding requested by the US after 
the Iran clash, cybersecurity stands at about 8% and is the largest dedicated capability line after munitions. Europe 
should match that signal. There is also a risk of new dependence on the US here. Leading AI models are ahead in 
detecting system flaws and patching them, and relying on them would hand a foreign provider a view into critical 
European systems. Europe should invest in open-weight models it can run, audit and adapt on its own infrastructure, 
and pair them with a European cyber shield. This is an under -funded segment and a strategic one: capacity that 
cannot be switched off from abroad.

A two-horizon roadmap 
Horizon 1 - Filling the short-term hole to secure protection minimum 
The most important action is being consistent. On the current path, spending reaches about 3.1% of GDP by 2035, 
close to EUR800bn a year. For a region that was below 2% only a few years ago, that is a strong result. The 3% 
objective may have been set too early, at a time when industry could not absorb the funds. The 3.5% and 5% paths 
show the stakes: they would require roughly EUR900bn and EUR1.3trn a year by 2035, volumes that factories and 
supply chains could not deliver on today’s footing. Policy should therefore be judged on more than one number. 
Four measures matter more. First, new output capacity: how many more missiles, vehicles and munitions Europe 
can produce each year. Second, industrial externalities for automotive, electronics, software and materials. Third, 
modernization: whether equipment and technology are up to date and are competitive for export market. Fourth, 
the reliance rate: the share of capability that depends on non -European suppliers. A budget that rises while these 
four indicators stagnate is a failure. A smaller one that raises  capacity and lowers dependence is a success. Europe 
should publish them alongside the spending ratio. 
Figure 27: EU-27 defense spending as % of GDP trajectory over 2026-2035 period

Sources: European Commission,  
European Defense Agency,  
Allianz Research  
Build the ground before the factories. Europe has bought the missiles and forgotten the ground they stand on. 
Infrastructure is where rearmament stalls: production sites, depots and logistics do not build themselves, and a 
sleepy private investment cycle will not wake without a push. Government s should provide it, through grants and 
subsidies aimed at defense infrastructure. NATO’s 1.5% of GDP line by 2035 should be read as a compass, not a 
deadline. Reaching it would mean covering a hole above EUR2trn, on top of procurement bills that are alrea dy 
heavy. Europe should set a realistic corridor of 0.5-1% instead, which still demands EUR760bn to EUR1.4trn of effort. 
Public money cannot replace the market, and it should not try. Its job is narrower: lower the entry cost, signal 
permanence and rekindle an investment engine slowed by high rates. Every euro of support should buy a 
commitment in return, on capacity, deadlines and European location. Defense becomes a new vertical of European 
industrial policy, planned with the ambition once reserved for energy and chips.

No free lunch: look at home before buying abroad . Europe’s car plants run below potential while its defen se lines 
run beyond it. This is an opportunity for European governments to play matchmaker and build up industrial bridges, 
especially as the automotive industry slows down . S teering idle automotive capacity, workforce and supplier 
networks toward defense primes in a first step, then lifting output without burning scarce capital would kill two birds 
with one stone: loosening  bottlenecks in defen se while easing the production cost structure for automakers . Each 
new link proves the two industrial agendas can be synchronized. Procurement must follow the same logic. Domestic 
orders come with capex commitments attached. Foreign technology is welcome only when the need is critical and 
recurrent, and only if it brings production and investment onto European soil. Buying abroad with no local return is 
a bill, not a strategy. The short -term priorities are narrow and clear: long -range missiles, air defen se to shield 
infrastructure and cyber tools against disinformation and external attacks. Europe should treat price as a security 
variable: build competing sources, pool orders to gain bargaining power and tie large contracts to production on 
European soil.

Flexibility is earned, not granted. The fiscal impulse is fading while rates are getting higher and stagflation risks in 
Europe are rising, which means public effort could weaken just when momentum is needed. Greater fiscal flexibility 
is required but  an extension of the national escape clause (NEC) is perhaps not the right answer to underpin a 
constant and robust investment cycle as results so far have been quite mixed. Incentives are missing and here is the 
crux of the capacity conundrum in Europe. Instead, a double approach in which a big military spender – either the 
state or a corporate - would be rewarded by a fiscal loosening measure and easier credit access  but support would 
be this time conditional and monitored against tangible results. Spending above budget lines would be authorized 
if, and only if, it is channeled into European-based industrial projects for identified critical equipment or resources , 
and key milestones are met on time. For firms, a tax-rebate of 1 to 2% could be applied in case of strong investment 
commitment that would be evaluate in function of their current capex polic ies and backlog level s. This should 
reassure sceptics who see another loosening of the rules as a loophole, and give markets what they want to see: 
ambition with discipline. Used well, a conditional waiver turns fiscal rules from an obstacle into an instrument of 
industrial strategy. Used badly, it becomes Europe’s next debt debate. 
Figure 29: Estimated 2027 output gap vs. fiscal pulse (based on primary budget balance as % of GDP)

Horizon 2: Thinking on a 2030 defensive line 
Building supranational reserves as the ultimate disruption-mitigation tool. Let’s take US as example. Having the 
biggest and most performing defense industry does not prevent it from building up strategic reserves to anticipate 
market shocks and/or a wartime-led demand surge. Europe should do the same for critical equipment, arms and 
ammunition. A reserve cuts dependence on foreign supply and buys time when conflict closes borders and ports. It 
also needs ownership beyond national flags. Build supranational governance a nd a shared financing base, with 
European borrowing on the table, so that every capital feels concerned. Condition ing financial support on joint 
procurement channels  would end the national conflicts of interest that have sunk projects before. The ideal is a  
nation-free fleet of arms and ammunition, usable only under NATO governance , with clear intermediate targets so 
that private firms can plan capacity, hire and invest with confidence.

Modernize or be dependent again... Modernization is the best protection against new dependence, and it protects 
what Europe sells best: defense exports are a major revenue line, and they erode the moment the continent slips off 
the technology curve. The priorities are clear. First, build an air -defense and drone industry at scale. Second, widen 
the footprint in space, with broader constellations and stronger launch capacity. Third, invest in AI, the most under -
funded defense segment, and fuse it with the cyber shield. Automation and robotics are the multiplier. Autonomous 
mobility advances from the car industry can transfer, and R&D should be pooled across sectors. Joint investment 
with semiconductor, software and base -metal producers should lift material quality, onboard intelligence and 
computing power. Quantum belongs in the same pipeline. The primary goal is a leading-edge European model that 
can be exported, not an assembly of imported parts.  
…and lose your best trade engine and political stabilizer . For years, aerospace and aircraft have been Europe's 
most performing segment in trade exchanges, generating a consistent EUR30–35bn annual surplus toward non-EU 
members. More recently, the ramp -up in defense capacity has added EUR1–3bn per year in weapons and 
ammunition exports, a contribution that is far from negligible. But this is not just about trade balances. European 
defense expertise is widely acknowledged abroad and serves as the most effective diplomatic connector during 
high-level political engagements with international partners. It is, in essence, a powerful geopolitical tool: defense 
equipment and know -how open doors to broader bilateral agreements, strengthen alliances and help counter 
global fragmentation. Failing to inves t in military modernization does not just weaken Europe's security – it risks 
dismantling the very engine that fuels its trade influence and strategic relevance on the world stage.

Drones and space are the bright spot, AI and robotics are under-invested. Europe’s defense technology is being 
rebuilt, but so far capital transmission has mostly being channeled where Europe was the weakest and political will 
was strongest: drones collected near half of defense -related investment in the European venture capital market 
(44% since 2020) , and space and satellite imagery follow. In the meantime, AI-for-defense has attracted almost 
nothing, and robotics only a fraction of drone funding. Yet warfare is shifting toward digital and  electric-powered 
assets. The economy runs on algorithms, data centers and power grids, which makes them primary targets, as this 
year’s Middle East conflict showed .  In this context, European governments and defense primes should (1) treat AI 
as a strategic asset and open procurement to outside technology firms; (2) use co -investment and guaranteed 
demand to channel private capital into robotics and autonomy, which should also ease the sector’s labor shortages, 
and (3) protect data centers and grids as defense infrastructure. The US and China are investing heavily in both 
fields and could turn that lead into a battlefield edge. Europe should not repeat in AI the delay it has just started to 
correct in drones.

Economy 
Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers 
09 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumer spending remained very solid in September. Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third strongest growth rates over the past four 
years. Month-over-month (MoM) spending was more muted, especially without gas. 
• Spending shows considerable breadth: the vast majority of discretionary retail and services categories are showing growth. 
Moreover, this strength extends across income cohorts, with the ongoing recovery of lower- and middle-income households' 
spending growth. 
• With the winter months fast approaching, holiday spending is already increasing at a solid rate, though it's still early. In Bank of 
America deposit data through September, buy now, pay later (BNPL) usage is rising but not at a rate that is overly concerning. 
However, nearly one in every five households that use BNPL has a repayment of over $500.

Consumer Checkpoint is a regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It aims to provide a holistic and real-time estimate of US 
consumers’ spending and their financial well-being, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America proprietary data. Such data is not 
intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, financial conditions or performance of 
Bank of America. 
A solid September for spending   
Consumer spending continued to look solid in September. Bank of America total aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), while spending excluding gasoline increased 5.1% YoY. Notably, both are the third 
strongest growth rates in the past four years. Seasonally-adjusted total card spending rose 0.2% month-over-month (MoM), 
while spending excluding gasoline was little changed. Overall, spending growth remains well above the 2025 average rate 
(Exhibit 1). 
Exhibit 1: Total card spending per household rose 6.2% YoY and 5.1% YoY excluding gasoline in September

(monthly, YoY%, non-SA, right-hand side (rhs))

Most spending categories are growing  
One striking thing about recent card spending, in our view, is that it is well balanced across categories. For example, both 
spending on retail (excluding gasoline) and services grew strongly YoY in September, at 3.5% and 5.4%, respectively. Meanwhile, 
retail spending is showing notably better momentum than in 2025 (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: Spending is rising across retail and services 
Retail (excluding gasoline) and services card spending per household (monthly, seasonally adjusted, index January 2025 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Well balanced across “nice-to-have” and “need-to-have” retail categories  
Is the momentum in retail spending reflective of “nice-to-have” discretionary or “need-to-have” necessity spending? In retail, 
this is a nuanced question, as many categories sit in a gray area between “necessity” and “discretionary”. General merchandise 
stores, for example, sell both essentials like groceries as well as nice-to-have electronics or even jewelry.  
In Exhibit 3, we account for this by grouping retail spending into “more discretionary” (furniture, electronics, building materials, 
online retailers, clothing retailers and miscellaneous stores), “partly discretionary” (auto, health and personal care retailers, 
general merchandise) and “mostly necessity” (gas and groceries). Even within these categories the edges will be blurred, but the 
overall impression is that retail spending growth is widely distributed, with spending rising across all fronts. One caveat: “mostly 
discretionary” spending has been making a smaller contribution since June. 
Exhibit 3: Retail spending is distributed across discretionary and necessity categories  
Percentage points contribution to YoY growth by type of retail spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data. See methodology for category aggregation definitions.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking at the share of “more discretionary” retail spending categories that are increasing YoY underscores that growth is well 
balanced and broad-based (Exhibit 4). Non-store and online retailers and miscellaneous stores saw some of the fastest growth, 
while furniture and building materials were among the laggards, though other durable categories showed some strength.

For services, growth is driven mostly by discretionary spending  
In services spending, on the other hand, the boundaries between discretionary and necessity spending are often clearer. Travel 
spending (e.g., airlines, lodging) can be thought of as, by and large, discretionary. At the same time, utility bills, rent and 
insurance payments are necessities.  
And in services, discretionary spending remains the clear driver, accounting for over three-quarters of YoY spending growth in 
September (Exhibit 6). Leisure and housing services are major components of growth, though travel and restaurants also made 
positive contributions. However, as we noted last month strength in airline spending largely reflects price rises rather than 
transactions growth (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling).

Lower-income households’ spending outpaced all but the top 5% 
The breadth in spending growth across categories is also evident across income cohorts. In September, lower-income 
households’ total card spending growth was 5.5% YoY, while the equivalent figures for middle- and higher-income households 
were 4.9% and 4.8% YoY, respectively. The top 5% of households by income continue to show the fastest spending growth, 
5.9% in September, though this has eased somewhat (Exhibit 7). 
Overall, the divergences across income cohorts are much smaller than they were in 2025 and the first three months of this year. 
Just focusing on spending excluding key necessities (gasoline, food and utilities) doesn’t meaningfully change the picture of 
convergence across income cohorts (Exhibit 8).  
Exhibit 7: The top 5% saw the strongest spending growth of any 
income cohort…

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The strength in lower- and middle-income households’ discretionary spending is occurring despite necessity spending shifting 
from a tailwind to a headwind over the past year amid rising gas prices (Exhibit 9). In our view, a key component of why these 
households have been able to combine rising necessity spending with strength in discretionary is the labor market and a 
recovery in their after-tax wage growth (Exhibit 10).  
In September, lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY from 4.7% YoY in August, while growth for middle- and higher-
income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. These changes were modest, and lower-income households are 
continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” pattern we 
observed over much of 2025 and 2026.

Should we be concerned about the easing in household spending and after-tax wage growth for those with the top 5% of 
incomes? For now, probably not, as in our view this spending growth is likely being driven by wealth effects rather than wage 
gains. Absent a sharp correction in equity markets, there is little reason to expect them to retrench their spending. However, 
developments here are worth watching as this cohort comprises a significant proportion of overall consumer spending. They also 
tend to make outsized contributions to several discretionary spending categories (Exhibit 11).

With summer gone, thoughts turn to the holidays and BNPL… 
Now that summer is over, the next big gauge of consumer momentum is how people spend during the run-up to, and over, the 
holiday season. It is early days, but September spending on holiday items ran well ahead of 2025 (Exhibit 12). Given the backdrop 
of firmer after-tax wage growth and breadth across retail and services categories, this is perhaps not surprising. However, some 
of this spending strength could be due to rising prices as opposed to more purchases.

A concern often raised as we move into the holidays is whether consumers will lean on less traditional forms of credit such as 
buy now, pay later (BNPL) to finance their spending. Frequent BNPL users tend to have higher credit card utilization rates (read 
more in: Do consumers have wiggle room to absorb higher gas prices?).  
Looking at the share of households in Bank of America deposit data with a BNPL payment, we do tend to see a seasonal rise 
over the holidays (Exhibit 13), though in past years, these increases have quickly reversed. Further, while the underlying trend in 
BNPL usage is upwards, there are no signs of a sharp acceleration. We do, however, see some evidence that BNPL is being used 
to finance more expensive purchases, with the share of households making repayments above $500 rising, albeit relatively slowly 
(Exhibit 14).
